
\medterm{P Wave} The first deflection on the ECG, representing atrial depolarization. Normal P waves are
upright in leads I and II, inverted in aVR, and less than 0.12 seconds (3 small boxes)
in duration and 2.5 mm in amplitude.

\medterm{Pacemaker, Artificial} An implanted device that sends electrical impulses to maintain an appropriate heart rhythm.

\medterm{Paediatrician}A physician specializing in the medical care of infants, children, and adolescents.
\medterm{Paediatrics}The medical specialty concerned with health and disease in infants, children, and adolescents.
\medterm{Pain Clinic}A service providing assessment and multidisciplinary treatment for acute, chronic, or complex pain.
\medterm{Palate}The roof of the mouth, consisting of a bony hard palate and muscular soft palate.
\medterm{Palatine}Relating to the palate or palatine bone.
\medterm{Paliperidone}An atypical antipsychotic medicine used to treat schizophrenia and schizoaffective disorder.
\medterm{Pallesthesia}The ability to perceive vibration, tested with a tuning fork and mediated by large sensory fibers.
\medterm{Palliative Surgery}Surgery intended to relieve symptoms or improve function without necessarily curing the underlying disease.
\medterm{Pallidotomy}A neurosurgical procedure creating a lesion in the globus pallidus to reduce selected symptoms of advanced Parkinson disease.
\medterm{Pallor}Unusual paleness of skin or mucous membranes, often due to reduced blood flow, anemia, or other illness.
\medterm{Palm}The inner surface of the hand between the wrist and fingers.
\medterm{Palmar}Relating to the palm of the hand.
\medterm{Palpebra}An eyelid.
\medterm{Palsy}Weakness or paralysis, often involving a specific nerve or muscle group.
\medterm{Panacea}A supposed remedy for all diseases; it is not a recognized medical treatment.
\medterm{Pancreas, Diseases of}Disorders affecting the pancreas, including pancreatitis, cysts, tumors, diabetes, and exocrine insufficiency.
\medterm{Pancreazymin}An older name for cholecystokinin, a hormone that stimulates pancreatic enzyme secretion and gallbladder contraction.
\medterm{Panendoscopy}Endoscopic examination of several connected parts of the upper aerodigestive tract, often the pharynx, larynx, and oesophagus.
\medterm{Panmyelopathy}Disease affecting multiple cell lines or functions of the bone marrow and spinal cord; the term is uncommon and context-dependent.
\medterm{Panophthalmitis}Severe inflammation involving all coats and internal contents of the eye, usually due to infection and threatening vision.
\medterm{Pantothenic Acid}Vitamin B5, a component of coenzyme A required for fatty-acid and energy metabolism.
\medterm{Papain}A proteolytic enzyme from papaya latex used in some food and historical medicinal preparations.
\medterm{Papanicolaou (Pap) Test}A screening test in which cervical cells are examined for precancerous changes and cancer.
\medterm{Papillectomy}Surgical removal of a papilla or papillary lesion, such as an ampullary tumor.
\medterm{Papilloedema}Swelling of the optic discs caused by raised intracranial pressure.
\medterm{Papillomavirus}A papillomavirus, especially human papillomavirus, which can cause warts and some anogenital, oropharyngeal, and other cancers.
\medterm{Papule}A small, solid, raised skin lesion, usually less than 1 centimeter in diameter.
\medterm{Para-}A combining form meaning beside, beyond, abnormal, or closely related, depending on the word.
\medterm{Paracusia}A disorder of hearing in which sounds are perceived abnormally or inaccurately.
\medterm{Paraesthesia}An abnormal sensation such as tingling, pins and needles, burning, or numbness.
\medterm{Paraffin}A mixture of hydrocarbons used in laboratory tissue processing, topical preparations, and other applications; some forms are flammable.
\medterm{Parageusia}An altered or unpleasant sense of taste.
\medterm{Paragraphia}A language disorder involving incorrect or unintended words or written symbols.
\medterm{Paraldehyde}An older sedative and anticonvulsant medicine with limited current use.
\medterm{Paralysis}Loss of voluntary muscle movement in part or all of the body due to nervous-system or muscle disease.
\medterm{Paralysis Agitans}An older name for Parkinson disease, characterized by tremor, rigidity, and bradykinesia.
\medterm{Paralytic Ileus}Failure of intestinal peristalsis without a mechanical blockage, causing abdominal distension, pain, vomiting, and absent or reduced bowel sounds.
\medterm{Paramedical}Relating to healthcare professionals or services that support medical practice, such as physiotherapy, radiography, or emergency medical care.
\medterm{Parametritis}Inflammation of the connective tissue beside the uterus, often following pelvic infection or childbirth.
\medterm{Paramnesia}A memory disturbance involving false, distorted, or inaccurately familiar recollections.
\medterm{Paranoid}Marked by persistent or excessive suspiciousness and distrust.
\medterm{Paranoid Psychosis}An older term for psychosis dominated by delusions of persecution, reference, or grandeur.
\medterm{Paranoid Schizophrenia}An obsolete subtype label for schizophrenia with prominent delusions and hallucinations; current classification uses symptom dimensions.
\medterm{Paraparesis}Weakness affecting both legs, usually due to spinal-cord, brain, nerve, or muscle disease.
\medterm{Paraphasia}Substitution, omission, or distortion of sounds or words during speech, usually due to aphasia.
\medterm{Paraplegia}Paralysis of both legs and often part of the trunk, usually from spinal-cord or neurologic disease.
\medterm{Paratyphoid Fever}An enteric fever caused by Salmonella Paratyphi, transmitted mainly through contaminated food or water.
\medterm{Parenchyma}The functional tissue of an organ, distinguished from its supporting connective tissue or stroma.
\medterm{Paresis}Partial weakness or incomplete paralysis.
\medterm{Paresthesia}An abnormal sensation such as tingling, prickling, burning, or pins and needles.
\medterm{Parietal}Relating to a wall of a body cavity or to the parietal bone or lobe of the skull and brain.
\medterm{Parietal Bone}One of two bones forming much of the sides and roof of the skull.
\medterm{Parotitis}Inflammation of the parotid salivary gland.
\medterm{Paroxysm}A sudden attack or burst of symptoms or physiologic activity.
\medterm{Paroxysmal Nocturnal Haemoglobinuria}An acquired clonal stem-cell disorder causing complement-mediated hemolysis, thrombosis, and variable bone-marrow failure.
\medterm{Pars}A part or portion of an anatomical structure, used in names such as pars interarticularis.
\medterm{Passive Immunity}Protection produced by transferred antibodies rather than by the person's own immune response, such as maternal antibodies or immunoglobulin treatment.
\medterm{Pasteurella}A genus of bacteria carried by animals that can cause wound infections after bites and other human disease.
\medterm{Pathognomonic}So characteristic of a disease that it strongly identifies that disease when present.
\medterm{Patient-Controlled Analgesia (PCA)}A system allowing a patient to self-administer preset doses of analgesic through a programmed pump with safety limits.
\medterm{Peak Flow Meter}A handheld device measuring peak expiratory flow, useful for monitoring variable airflow obstruction such as asthma.
\medterm{Pectoral}Relating to the chest or breast.
\medterm{Pectoralis}A muscle of the chest, chiefly pectoralis major or pectoralis minor.
\medterm{Pediculosis}Infestation by lice.
\medterm{Pedigree}A diagram showing family relationships and inheritance of traits or disease.
\medterm{Pedodontics}The branch of dentistry concerned with infants, children, and adolescents.
\medterm{Pellagra}A disease caused by niacin deficiency, classically producing dermatitis, diarrhea, and neuropsychiatric changes.
\medterm{Pelvimeter}An instrument used to measure pelvic dimensions.
\medterm{Pelvis}The bony ring and associated soft tissues at the base of the trunk, connecting the spine with the lower limbs.
\medterm{Pemphigoid}A group of autoimmune subepidermal blistering disorders, including bullous pemphigoid.
\medterm{Pemphigus}A group of autoimmune intraepidermal blistering disorders caused by antibodies against desmosomal proteins.
\medterm{Pendular}Moving or swinging freely like a pendulum; pendular nystagmus has a similar speed in both directions.
\medterm{Penetrating Wounds}Wounds caused by an object entering tissue and potentially damaging deeper structures.
\medterm{Penicillamine}A chelating and immunomodulatory medicine used in selected copper overload and autoimmune disorders.
\medterm{Penicillin}A beta-lactam antibiotic group that inhibits bacterial cell-wall synthesis.
\medterm{Penis}The male external genital organ used for urination and sexual reproduction.
\medterm{Pentaerythritol Tetranitrate}An organic nitrate with vasodilator activity, mainly of historical or specialized pharmaceutical interest.
\medterm{Pentamidine}An antiprotozoal and antimicrobial medicine used for selected leishmaniasis, trypanosomiasis, and Pneumocystis infections.
\medterm{Pentazocine}An opioid analgesic with mixed agonist-antagonist activity.
\medterm{Pentobarbital}A barbiturate sedative and anticonvulsant with substantial respiratory-depression and overdose risk.
\medterm{Peppermint}Mentha piperita, used as a flavoring and in some preparations for dyspeptic symptoms; concentrated oil can be hazardous.
\medterm{Pepsinogen}An inactive precursor released by gastric chief cells and converted to pepsin in acidic conditions.
\medterm{Peptic Ulcer}A break in the lining of the stomach or duodenum caused commonly by Helicobacter pylori or NSAIDs.
\medterm{Peptidase}An enzyme that breaks peptide bonds in proteins or peptides.
\medterm{Peptide}A short chain of amino acids linked by peptide bonds.
\medterm{Perceptual}Relating to the process of detecting and interpreting sensory information.
\medterm{Percussion}Tapping the body surface to assess the underlying tissue by its sound and transmitted vibration.
\medterm{Percutaneous}Performed through the skin, usually with a needle, catheter, or other instrument.
\medterm{Perforation}A hole or rupture through the wall of an organ or tissue.
\medterm{Perfusion}Passage of blood or another fluid through a tissue or organ.
\medterm{Pericardial Effusion}Excess fluid in the pericardial sac surrounding the heart; rapid accumulation can cause tamponade.
\medterm{Pericarditis}Inflammation of the pericardium, often causing sharp chest pain and characteristic positional changes.
\medterm{Pericardium}The double-layered sac surrounding the heart and the roots of the great vessels.
\medterm{Perichondrium}The connective-tissue covering surrounding most cartilage.
\medterm{Perineum}The region between the external genitalia and anus.
\medterm{Periodontal Disease}Inflammatory or infectious disease of the tissues supporting the teeth, including gingivitis and periodontitis.
\medterm{Periodontics}The dental specialty concerned with the gums and supporting tissues of the teeth.
\medterm{Periodontitis} A chronic inflammatory disease caused by dysbiotic dental plaque that destroys the periodontal ligament and alveolar bone supporting the teeth. It can cause bleeding gums, periodontal pockets, tooth mobility, recession, bad breath, and tooth loss; severity and progression vary and require dental evaluation.
\medterm{Periodontium}The tissues supporting a tooth: gingiva, periodontal ligament, cementum, and alveolar bone.
\medterm{Periosteum}A vascular connective-tissue membrane covering the outer surface of bone except at articular cartilage.
\medterm{Periostitis}Inflammation of the periosteum.
\medterm{Periurethral}Situated around the urethra.
\medterm{Perleche}Angular cheilitis, inflammation and cracking at the corners of the mouth.
\medterm{Permanent Teeth}The adult set of teeth that replaces the deciduous teeth.
\medterm{Permethrin}A topical pyrethroid medicine used mainly to treat scabies and head lice.
\medterm{Pernicious Anaemia}An autoimmune cause of vitamin B12 deficiency due to impaired intrinsic-factor-mediated absorption.
\medterm{Peroneal}Relating to the fibula or the muscles and nerve on the outer side of the lower leg.
\medterm{Perphenazine}A phenothiazine antipsychotic and antiemetic medicine.
\medterm{Perspiration}Sweat produced by eccrine and apocrine glands.
\medterm{Pertussis}Whooping cough, a contagious respiratory infection caused by Bordetella pertussis.
\medterm{Pervasive Developmental Disorders}An obsolete group term for disorders involving social communication and restricted repetitive behavior, including autism-spectrum conditions.
\medterm{Pessary}A device placed in the vagina to support pelvic organs or deliver medication.
\medterm{Pesticide}A substance used to control insects, weeds, fungi, or other pests; exposure can cause poisoning.
\medterm{PET Scan}An imaging study using a positron-emitting tracer to measure metabolism, receptor activity, or tissue function.
\medterm{Petechiae}Tiny nonblanching red or purple spots caused by small areas of bleeding into the skin or mucosa.
\medterm{Petit Mal}An older term for absence seizures, brief episodes of impaired awareness with characteristic generalized EEG activity.
\medterm{Petrissage}A massage technique involving kneading or lifting soft tissues.
\medterm{Petrolatum}A semisolid mixture of hydrocarbons used as a protective skin ointment and moisture barrier.
\medterm{Petrous}Dense and rock-like; usually referring to the petrous part of the temporal bone.
\medterm{Phagocyte}A cell that engulfs and digests microorganisms, dead cells, or foreign particles.
\medterm{Phagocytosis}The process by which a cell surrounds, ingests, and digests a particle or microorganism.
\medterm{Phalanges}The bones of the fingers and toes.
\medterm{Phalanx}One of the bones of a finger or toe.
\medterm{Phallic Stage}A historical psychoanalytic developmental stage centered on genital awareness; it is not a modern disease classification.
\medterm{Pharmaceutical}Relating to medicines or the preparation and manufacture of medicines.
\medterm{Pharmacist}A healthcare professional trained in medicines, including their preparation, dispensing, safety, and use.
\medterm{Pharmacodynamics} The study of what a drug does to the body, including its molecular targets, mechanism of action, dose-response relationship, therapeutic effects, and adverse effects.
\medterm{Pharmacokinetics} The study of what the body does to a drug, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME), and how drug concentrations change over time.
\medterm{Pharmacology}The study of drugs, their actions, uses, adverse effects, and interactions.
\medterm{Pharmacy}The science and practice of preparing, supplying, and advising on medicines.
\medterm{Pharyngeal}Relating to the pharynx.
\medterm{Pharyngitis}Inflammation of the pharynx, commonly causing sore throat and pain on swallowing.
\medterm{Pharynx}The muscular throat passage behind the nose and mouth that leads to the larynx and oesophagus.
\medterm{Phenelzine}A monoamine oxidase inhibitor antidepressant requiring attention to interactions and dietary precautions.
\medterm{Phenindione}An older oral anticoagulant that interferes with vitamin-K-dependent clotting factors.
\medterm{Phenobarbitone}The British name for phenobarbital, a barbiturate anticonvulsant and sedative.
\medterm{Phenol}A corrosive aromatic compound used in chemical and laboratory applications and in some medical preparations.
\medterm{Phenomenon}An observable event, sign, or process.
\medterm{Phenothiazines}A class of medicines including antipsychotic and antiemetic drugs.
\medterm{Phenoxybenzamine}An irreversible alpha-adrenergic blocker used mainly before surgery for pheochromocytoma.
\medterm{Phenoxymethylpenicillin}Penicillin V, an oral penicillin used for selected susceptible infections.
\medterm{Phentermine}A sympathomimetic appetite suppressant used short term in selected weight-management plans.
\medterm{Phenylbutazone}An older NSAID with serious blood, kidney, gastrointestinal, and other toxicity risks.
\medterm{Phenylephrine}An alpha-adrenergic agonist used as a decongestant, vasopressor, or pupil dilator.
\medterm{Phenylketonuria (PKU)}An inherited deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase causing phenylalanine accumulation and neurologic injury without early treatment.
\medterm{Phenytoin}An anticonvulsant that blocks voltage-gated sodium channels and treats selected seizures.
\medterm{Pheochromocytoma}A catecholamine-producing tumor of chromaffin cells, usually in the adrenal medulla, causing episodic or sustained hypertension.
\medterm{Phimosis}Inability to retract the foreskin behind the glans; pathologic cases may cause pain, infection, or urinary difficulty.
\medterm{Phlebitis}Inflammation of a vein, sometimes associated with thrombosis.
\medterm{Phlegm}Thick mucus coughed up from the lower respiratory tract.
\medterm{Phobia}An excessive, persistent fear of a specific object or situation that leads to avoidance or distress.
\medterm{Phobic Disorder}A disorder characterized by clinically significant fear and avoidance of a particular object or situation.
\medterm{Phocomelia}A rare congenital limb-development defect in which hands or feet are attached close to the trunk because of absent or severely shortened long bones.
\medterm{Phonocardiogram}A graphic recording of heart sounds and murmurs.
\medterm{Phosphate}A salt or ester of phosphoric acid; phosphate is important in bone, energy metabolism, and cellular signaling.
\medterm{Phospholipid}A lipid containing phosphate that forms a major component of cell membranes.
\medterm{Phosphorus}An essential element in bone, nucleic acids, phospholipids, and ATP.
\medterm{Phosphorylation}Addition of a phosphate group to a molecule, often regulating protein activity or energy transfer.
\medterm{Photophobia}Abnormal sensitivity or intolerance to light.
\medterm{Phototherapy}Treatment using controlled light, such as for neonatal jaundice, psoriasis, or seasonal affective disorder.
\medterm{Phrenic}Relating to the diaphragm or the phrenic nerve.
\medterm{Phrenic Nerve}The nerve arising mainly from C3-C5 that supplies the diaphragm and carries sensory fibers from nearby structures.
\medterm{Phthiriasis}Infestation by lice, especially the pubic louse.
\medterm{Phthisis}An obsolete term for wasting or shrinkage of an organ, especially a severely damaged eye or lung.
\medterm{Physical Medicine}The medical specialty focused on function, rehabilitation, pain, and restoration of independence after illness or injury.
\medterm{Physician}A licensed medical doctor who diagnoses, treats, and prevents disease.
\medterm{Physiotherapy}Assessment and treatment using movement, exercise, manual techniques, education, and physical modalities to improve function.
\medterm{Physiology}The study of normal functions and processes of living organisms and their parts.
\medterm{Pia Mater}The delicate innermost membrane closely covering the brain and spinal cord.
\medterm{Pica}Persistent eating of nonnutritive substances inappropriate for developmental level or culture.
\medterm{Pick's Disease}An older term for frontotemporal dementia associated with characteristic Pick bodies; current classification uses specific frontotemporal dementia syndromes.
\medterm{Pickwickian Syndrome}An older name for obesity hypoventilation syndrome, involving obesity, daytime hypercapnia, and sleep-disordered breathing.
\medterm{Pilocarpine}A muscarinic agonist used to constrict the pupil, lower intraocular pressure, or stimulate salivation.
\medterm{Pimozide}An antipsychotic medicine used in selected disorders, with important cardiac and neurologic adverse effects.
\medterm{Pimple}A common lay term for a small inflamed or blocked skin lesion, often an acne papule or pustule.
\medterm{Pineal Gland}An endocrine gland in the brain that secretes melatonin and helps regulate circadian timing.
\medterm{Pink Eye}A lay term for conjunctivitis, inflammation of the conjunctiva causing redness and discharge.
\medterm{Pinna}The visible external part of the ear that collects sound.
\medterm{Pinocytosis}Cellular uptake of extracellular fluid and dissolved substances in small vesicles.
\medterm{Pint}A unit of volume; its exact value varies by measurement system and country.
\medterm{Pinta}A chronic skin infection caused by Treponema carateum, producing changing stages of pigmentary lesions.
\medterm{Pinworm}The intestinal nematode Enterobius vermicularis, which commonly causes nocturnal perianal itching.
\medterm{Piperazine}An older anthelmintic used against selected intestinal worms.
\medterm{Pipotiazine}A long-acting phenothiazine antipsychotic administered in selected settings.
\medterm{Pipradrol}An older central nervous system stimulant with limited current use.
\medterm{Piracetam}A nootropic compound used in some countries for selected neurologic indications; evidence and approval vary.
\medterm{Piroplasmosis}An animal disease caused by Babesia or Theileria parasites; human babesiosis is a related infection.
\medterm{Pits}Small depressions or holes; in dermatology, pits may occur in nails or skin lesions.
\medterm{Pituitary Gland}An endocrine gland at the base of the brain that regulates growth, thyroid, adrenal, reproductive, lactation, and water-balance hormones.
\medterm{Pityriasis}A group of skin disorders characterized by fine scaling, including pityriasis rosea and versicolor.
\medterm{Placebo}An inactive or nonspecific treatment used as a comparison or whose effects arise from expectation and care context.
\medterm{Placenta}The temporary organ connecting mother and fetus, allowing exchange of oxygen, nutrients, wastes, and hormones.
\medterm{Placenta Praevia}Placental implantation over or near the cervical opening, causing risk of painless late-pregnancy bleeding.
\medterm{Plague}A serious infection caused by Yersinia pestis, transmitted mainly by infected fleas or respiratory droplets in pneumonic disease.
\medterm{Planar}Flat or lying in a single plane; planar imaging produces a two-dimensional projection.
\medterm{Plantar}Relating to the sole of the foot.
\medterm{Plantar Reflex}The response produced by stroking the sole of the foot; an extensor response in an adult suggests corticospinal-tract dysfunction.
\medterm{Plantar Wart}A wart caused by human papillomavirus on the sole of the foot, often painful with pressure.
\medterm{Plaque}A flat, raised, or deposited area of material or tissue; examples include dental plaque, arterial plaque, and skin plaques.
\medterm{Plasma Expanders}Intravenous fluids intended to increase circulating volume, including crystalloids and selected colloids.
\medterm{Plasmacytoma}A localized tumor of abnormal plasma cells, occurring in bone or soft tissue and sometimes progressing to multiple myeloma.
\medterm{Plasmapheresis}Removal of plasma from blood, with return of cellular components and replacement fluid, to remove pathogenic antibodies or substances.
\medterm{Plasmodium}A genus of parasites transmitted by Anopheles mosquitoes and responsible for malaria.
\medterm{Plaster of Paris}Calcium sulfate hemihydrate that sets into a hard material, used for casts, splints, and models.
\medterm{Plastic Surgery}Surgery that reconstructs, restores, or alters body tissues for functional or cosmetic reasons.
\medterm{Platelet}A small blood cell fragment that helps form the initial plug in hemostasis.
\medterm{Platelet Count}The number of platelets in a given volume of blood, used to assess bleeding or clotting risk.
\medterm{Platybasia}Flattening of the skull base, measured by an increased basal angle and sometimes associated with neurologic compression.
\medterm{Platysma}A thin superficial muscle of the neck that tenses the skin and assists lower-jaw and lower-lip movement.
\medterm{Pleat}A fold of tissue or material; in anatomy it may describe a fold of mucosa or serosa.
\medterm{Pleura}The double-layered membrane surrounding each lung and lining the inner chest wall.
\medterm{Pleural Effusion}Excess fluid in the pleural space between the lung and chest wall.
\medterm{Pleurisy}Inflammation of the pleura, often causing sharp pain worsened by breathing.
\medterm{Pleurodynia}Severe, often episodic chest or upper-abdominal muscle pain associated with viral infection, especially coxsackievirus.
\medterm{Plexus}A network of intersecting nerves, vessels, or other structures.
\medterm{Plica}A fold of tissue, especially a synovial fold within a joint.
\medterm{Plummer-Vinson Syndrome}Iron-deficiency anemia with dysphagia and esophageal webs, associated with increased upper gastrointestinal cancer risk.
\medterm{Pluripotential}Capable of differentiating into many, but not all, cell types.
\medterm{Plutonium}A radioactive heavy metal whose inhalation or ingestion can cause internal radiation exposure and toxicity.
\medterm{Pm}An abbreviation that may mean afternoon, post-mortem, or picometer; meaning depends on clinical context.
\medterm{Pneumatic}Operating by air or gas pressure.
\medterm{Pneumatic Tourniquet}An inflatable cuff used to temporarily reduce blood flow to a limb during surgery or selected procedures.
\medterm{Pneumatocele}A thin-walled, air-filled cavity in the lung, often developing after infection or trauma.
\medterm{Pneumatosis}Presence of gas within a tissue or organ wall, such as pneumatosis intestinalis.
\medterm{Pneumoconiosis}A group of occupational lung diseases caused by inhalation of mineral or industrial dusts.
\medterm{Pneumocystis Carinii Pneumonia}The historical name for Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, an opportunistic lung infection in immunocompromised people.
\medterm{Pneumocyte}An epithelial cell lining the alveoli; type I cells exchange gases and type II cells produce surfactant.
\medterm{Pneumoencephalography}An obsolete imaging procedure that replaced cerebrospinal fluid with air to outline the brain on X-ray.
\medterm{Pneumolysis}Surgical separation or freeing of lung tissue or pleural adhesions.
\medterm{Pneumomediastinum}Air within the mediastinum, caused by trauma, rupture, barotrauma, or air tracking from the lungs or neck.
\medterm{Pneumonectomy}Surgical removal of an entire lung.
\medterm{Pneumonia}Infection or inflammation of lung tissue causing symptoms such as cough, fever, breathlessness, and abnormal imaging.
\medterm{Pneumonitis}Inflammation of lung tissue, often from immune, drug, radiation, aspiration, or environmental causes rather than infection.
\medterm{Pneumoperitoneum}Air or gas within the peritoneal cavity, commonly from perforated bowel or abdominal surgery.
\medterm{Pneumothorax}Air in the pleural space causing partial or complete lung collapse.
\medterm{Podagra}Acute gout affecting the first metatarsophalangeal joint, causing severe pain, redness, and swelling.
\medterm{Podiatrist}A healthcare professional specializing in disorders of the feet and lower limbs.
\medterm{Podiatry}The healthcare field concerned with the feet, ankles, and related lower-limb disorders.
\medterm{Podophyllum}A plant genus containing podophyllotoxin, used in some topical wart treatments but toxic if misused.
\medterm{Poikilocytosis}Variation in the shape of red blood cells.
\medterm{Poikilotherm}An organism whose body temperature varies substantially with environmental temperature; humans are homeothermic.
\medterm{Point}A specific location, spot, or value; in medicine it may refer to a tender point, pressure point, or measurement point.
\medterm{Poison}A substance capable of causing injury, illness, or death when absorbed in sufficient amount.
\medterm{Poisoning}Harmful effects caused by exposure to a toxic substance.
\medterm{Poisson Distribution}A probability distribution modeling the number of events occurring in a fixed interval when events occur independently at a stable average rate.
\medterm{Polar}Relating to a pole, direction, or molecule with separated electrical charges.
\medterm{Polarity}The distribution or orientation of positive and negative electrical or biological properties.
\medterm{Poliomyelitis} An infection caused by poliovirus, usually spread by the fecal-oral route. Most infections are asymptomatic or cause a mild febrile illness, but some cause meningitis or damage to anterior-horn motor neurons, resulting in acute asymmetric flaccid paralysis and sometimes permanent weakness.
\medterm{Politzerization}A historical method of inflating the middle ear through the nose during swallowing to improve Eustachian-tube ventilation.
\medterm{Pollakiuria}Abnormally frequent urination, often in small amounts.
\medterm{Pollen}Fine grains released by plants that can trigger allergic rhinitis, asthma, or conjunctivitis.
\medterm{Pollex}The thumb.
\medterm{Pollution}Contamination of air, water, soil, or surroundings by substances that may harm health or ecosystems.
\medterm{Poly-}A combining form meaning many or much.
\medterm{Polyarthritis}Inflammation or disease affecting multiple joints.
\medterm{Polyarteritis}Inflammation of multiple arteries; polyarteritis nodosa is a systemic necrotizing vasculitis.
\medterm{Polychromasia}Presence of immature, bluish red blood cells in peripheral blood, reflecting increased marrow release.
\medterm{Polycystic Kidney Disease}An inherited disorder in which numerous kidney cysts enlarge the kidneys and may cause hypertension and renal failure.
\medterm{Polycystic Ovary Syndrome (PCOS)}A common endocrine disorder involving ovulatory dysfunction, androgen excess, and polycystic ovarian morphology in varying combinations.
\medterm{Polycythaemia}An increased red-cell mass or concentration, which may be primary or secondary to another condition.
\medterm{Polydactyly}Presence of extra fingers or toes.
\medterm{Polydipsia}Excessive thirst or drinking.
\medterm{Polyethylene Glycol}A polymer used as an osmotic laxative, excipient, and in various medical products.
\medterm{Polygenic}Influenced by or caused by variants in multiple genes.
\medterm{Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR)}A laboratory method that amplifies selected DNA sequences for detecting organisms or genetic variants.
\medterm{Polymagia}Excessive hunger or eating.
\medterm{Polymastia}The presence of extra breast tissue or additional nipples.
\medterm{Polymenorrhoea}Menstrual bleeding occurring at unusually short intervals.
\medterm{Polymorphism}The occurrence of two or more genetic forms or phenotypes in a population.
\medterm{Polymyxin}A group of antibiotics that disrupt bacterial cell membranes, used mainly for selected gram-negative infections.
\medterm{Polymyalgia Rheumatica}An inflammatory disorder causing aching and morning stiffness around the shoulders and hips in older adults.
\medterm{Polymyositis}An autoimmune inflammatory muscle disease causing symmetric proximal muscle weakness.
\medterm{Polypeptide}A chain of amino acids linked by peptide bonds.
\medterm{Polyposis}A condition in which numerous polyps develop, especially in the gastrointestinal tract.
\medterm{Polyradiculitis}Inflammation or disease affecting multiple spinal nerve roots.
\medterm{Polysaccharide}A carbohydrate composed of many linked monosaccharide units.
\medterm{Polysomia}The presence of extra copies of one or more chromosomes or, in developmental terminology, multiple body structures.
\medterm{Polythelia}The presence of one or more additional nipples.
\medterm{Polyuria}Excessive urine production, commonly more than about 3 liters per day in an adult.
\medterm{Polyvalent}Effective against or involving multiple types, strains, antigens, or targets.
\medterm{Pompholyx}A form of dyshidrotic eczema causing intensely itchy small blisters on the hands or feet.
\medterm{Pons}The middle part of the brainstem, containing pathways and nuclei involved in movement, sensation, breathing, and cranial-nerve function.
\medterm{Pontine}Relating to the pons.
\medterm{Popliteal}Relating to the back of the knee.
\medterm{Pore}A small opening in the skin or another surface, often associated with a gland or follicle.
\medterm{Porencephaly}A cavity or cyst within the brain, usually caused by developmental abnormality or prenatal or acquired injury.
\medterm{Pork Tapeworm}Taenia solium, a tapeworm acquired from undercooked pork; its larvae can cause cysticercosis.
\medterm{Porphria}A misspelling of porphyria, a group of disorders involving abnormal heme synthesis.
\medterm{Porphyria}A group of inherited or acquired disorders caused by defects in heme biosynthesis, producing neurologic, abdominal, or skin symptoms.
\medterm{Porphyrin}A ring-shaped compound involved in heme and other biological molecules; abnormal accumulation occurs in porphyrias.
\medterm{Port-Wine Stain}A permanent capillary malformation producing a flat pink, red, or purple skin patch.
\medterm{Position}The location or posture of a body part, organ, fetus, or patient.
\medterm{Positive Feedback}A regulatory process in which an output amplifies the process that produced it, as in labor contractions.
\medterm{Positron Emission Tomography (PET)}Imaging that detects paired photons from a positron-emitting tracer to map metabolism or molecular activity.
\medterm{Post-}A combining form meaning after or behind.
\medterm{Post-Concussional Syndrome}Persistent physical, cognitive, emotional, or sleep symptoms after a concussion or mild traumatic brain injury.
\medterm{Posterior}Situated behind or toward the back of the body or a structure.
\medterm{Post-Hoc}Performed or analyzed after an event; in statistics, a post-hoc analysis follows the planned primary analysis.
\medterm{Posthumous}Occurring or published after a person's death.
\medterm{Post-Mortem}Occurring after death or relating to examination of a body after death.
\medterm{Postnatal}Relating to the period after birth.
\medterm{Postpartum Depression}A depressive episode occurring during pregnancy or after childbirth, causing significant distress or impairment.
\medterm{Postpartum Haemorrhage}Excessive bleeding after childbirth, commonly defined by blood loss of at least 1,000 mL or bleeding with hypovolemia after birth.
\medterm{Post-Prandial}Occurring after a meal.
\medterm{Postural Drainage}Positioning a person so gravity assists drainage of secretions from selected parts of the lungs.
\medterm{Postural Hypotension}A significant fall in blood pressure on standing, often causing dizziness or fainting.
\medterm{Posture}The alignment and position of the body at rest or during activity.
\medterm{Potassium}An essential intracellular electrolyte important for nerve conduction, muscle contraction, and cardiac rhythm.
\medterm{Potassium Permanganate}An oxidizing antiseptic used in very dilute topical solutions; concentrated material can burn tissue.
\medterm{Potassium-Sparing Diuretic}A diuretic that promotes sodium excretion while reducing potassium loss, such as spironolactone or amiloride.
\medterm{Pott's Disease}Tuberculosis of the spine, causing vertebral destruction, back pain, deformity, or neurologic compromise.
\medterm{Pott's Fracture}An ankle fracture-dislocation involving the malleoli, historically defined variably.
\medterm{Pouch}A sac-like anatomical structure or surgically created reservoir.
\medterm{Poultice}A soft, moist substance applied to the body, historically to soothe or draw fluid from tissue.
\medterm{Pound}A unit of weight or pressure; the exact meaning depends on context.
\medterm{Poverty}Insufficient material resources to meet basic needs, a social determinant strongly affecting health and access to care.
\medterm{Pox}A disease producing vesicles, pustules, or pitted scars; the term is used in names such as smallpox and cowpox.
\medterm{Poxvirus}A family of large DNA viruses that includes variola, vaccinia, mpox, and molluscum contagiosum viruses.
\medterm{Practitioner}A person qualified and authorized to practice a healthcare profession.
\medterm{Pragmatic}Focused on practical outcomes or real-world effectiveness.
\medterm{Pramipexole}A dopamine agonist used mainly for Parkinson disease and restless legs syndrome.
\medterm{Prasugrel}An antiplatelet P2Y12 inhibitor used in selected patients with acute coronary syndrome undergoing PCI.
\medterm{Pravastatin}A statin that lowers LDL cholesterol by inhibiting HMG-CoA reductase.
\medterm{Praziquantel}An anthelmintic used for schistosomiasis and several tapeworm and fluke infections.
\medterm{Pre-}A combining form meaning before or in front of.
\medterm{Pre-Cancerous}Showing cellular changes that may progress to cancer but are not invasive cancer.
\medterm{Precaution}An action taken to reduce risk of harm, infection, or error.
\medterm{Preclinical}Before human clinical testing or relating to disease changes before symptoms become evident.
\medterm{Precocious Puberty}Onset of secondary sexual characteristics earlier than the accepted normal age range.
\medterm{Pre-Eclampsia}New-onset hypertension after 20 weeks of pregnancy with proteinuria or other maternal organ or placental dysfunction.
\medterm{Prefrontal}Relating to the anterior part of the frontal lobe involved in planning, judgment, working memory, and behavior.
\medterm{Pregnancy Test}A test detecting human chorionic gonadotropin in urine or blood to identify pregnancy.
\medterm{Preleukaemia}An obsolete term formerly used for myelodysplastic syndromes or other states preceding leukemia.
\medterm{Premalignant}Having cellular or tissue changes that may progress to invasive cancer.
\medterm{Premature}Occurring earlier than expected or before the usual developmental or gestational time.
\medterm{Premature Ejaculation}Ejaculation that occurs sooner than desired with poor control and causes persistent personal or relationship distress.
\medterm{Premature Infant}An infant born before 37 completed weeks of gestation.
\medterm{Premature Menopause}Loss of ovarian function and menstruation before age 40, now often termed primary ovarian insufficiency.
\medterm{Premenstrual Dysphoric Disorder (PMDD)}A severe cyclical mood disorder with affective and physical symptoms in the luteal phase that remit after menstruation.
\medterm{Premenstrual Syndrome (PMS)}Recurring physical, emotional, or behavioral symptoms before menstruation that resolve around or after its onset.
\medterm{Premolar}A tooth between the canine and molar teeth.
\medterm{Prepuce}The fold of skin covering the glans penis or clitoris; penile prepuce is the foreskin.
\medterm{Presbyacusis}Age-related progressive sensorineural hearing loss, usually affecting high frequencies.
\medterm{Presbyopia}Age-related loss of near focusing caused by reduced lens accommodation.
\medterm{Prescription}A written or electronic instruction from an authorized prescriber for a medicine or treatment.
\medterm{Presenile Dementia}An older term for dementia beginning before old age; modern diagnosis specifies the cause and syndrome.
\medterm{Presentation}The part of a fetus that is directed toward the birth canal, or the symptoms and signs with which a patient seeks care.
\medterm{Preservation}Maintenance of tissue, cells, organs, or function by preventing deterioration.
\medterm{Preservative}A substance added to prevent microbial growth or chemical deterioration of a product.
\medterm{Pressor}Raising blood pressure, usually by constricting blood vessels or increasing cardiac output.
\medterm{Pressure}Force exerted over an area; clinically measured in units such as mmHg.
\medterm{Pressure Sore}A localized injury to skin and underlying tissue caused by prolonged pressure or shear, also called a pressure ulcer.
\medterm{Pre-Synaptic}Situated before a synapse, especially the nerve-terminal side that releases a neurotransmitter.
\medterm{Pretibial Myxoedema}Thickened, waxy swelling of the skin over the shins, most often associated with Graves disease.
\medterm{Prevalence}The proportion of a population with a disease or characteristic at a specified time or period.
\medterm{Prevention}Measures intended to avoid disease, detect it early, limit complications, or reduce disability.
\medterm{Preventive Medicine}The field concerned with preventing disease and promoting health in individuals and populations.
\medterm{Priapism}A persistent, usually painful erection unrelated to sexual stimulation and lasting long enough to require urgent assessment.
\medterm{Prickle Cell}An obsolete or imprecise term sometimes used for a dendritic epidermal cell or for a sickle cell; the intended meaning requires context.
\medterm{Prickly Heat}Miliaria, an itchy rash caused by blockage of sweat ducts.
\medterm{Primary}First in time, origin, importance, or site; in disease classification it often means arising independently rather than secondary to another cause.
\medterm{Primary Care}First-contact, continuing, comprehensive healthcare, including prevention and management of common conditions.
\medterm{Primipara}A person who has given birth once after the stage of fetal viability.
\medterm{Primitive}Relating to an early developmental stage or a basic, less differentiated form.
\medterm{Primordial}Existing at the earliest stage of development.
\medterm{Prion}An abnormally folded infectious protein that can cause progressive neurodegenerative disease.
\medterm{Prism}A transparent optical device that bends light; prisms may correct or measure ocular alignment.
\medterm{PR Interval}The ECG interval from the start of atrial depolarization to the start of ventricular depolarization, reflecting atrioventricular conduction.
\medterm{Pro-}A combining form meaning before, forward, or in favor of.
\medterm{Probenecid}A uricosuric medicine used to prevent gout attacks in selected patients and to alter renal excretion of some drugs.
\medterm{Prob Problem}An incomplete or nonstandard label; its intended clinical meaning cannot be determined without source context.
\medterm{Problem}A symptom, sign, diagnosis, or issue requiring assessment or management.
\medterm{Probocis}A misspelling of proboscis, a projecting snout or appendage.
\medterm{Proboscis}An elongated nose, snout, or tubular feeding organ.
\medterm{Procainamide}An antiarrhythmic medicine used for selected cardiac rhythm disorders.
\medterm{Procaine}An ester local anesthetic, now used mainly in limited or historical settings.
\medterm{Procarbazine}An alkylating-like chemotherapy medicine used in selected lymphomas and brain tumors.
\medterm{Prochlorperazine}A phenothiazine medicine used as an antiemetic and antipsychotic.
\medterm{Proctalgia}Pain in or around the anus or rectum.
\medterm{Proctitis}Inflammation of the rectal lining.
\medterm{Proctoclysis}Administration of fluid into the rectum, an obsolete method of fluid or medicine delivery.
\medterm{Proctology}The medical specialty concerned with diseases of the anus and rectum.
\medterm{Proctoscope}An instrument used to inspect the anal canal and rectum.
\medterm{Proctoscopy}Examination of the anal canal and rectum with a proctoscope.
\medterm{Proctostomy}Surgical creation of an opening from the rectum to the skin.
\medterm{Prodrome}An early symptom or group of symptoms preceding a disease or major clinical event.
\medterm{Productive Cough}A cough that brings up sputum or phlegm.
\medterm{Profession}A skilled occupation requiring specialized education and ethical standards.
\medterm{Professional}Relating to a trained occupation or to the conduct expected of a qualified practitioner.
\medterm{Profunda}A deep artery or vessel, commonly referring to the profunda femoris artery.
\medterm{Progenitor}A cell that has partially differentiated and can give rise to one or more mature cell types.
\medterm{Progeny}Offspring or descendants.
\medterm{Progeria}A rare genetic disorder causing features of accelerated aging beginning in childhood.
\medterm{Progestational}Relating to progesterone-like effects or to preparation of the uterus for pregnancy.
\medterm{Progesterone}A steroid hormone produced mainly by the corpus luteum and placenta that supports the endometrium and pregnancy.
\medterm{Progestogen}A natural or synthetic hormone with progesterone-like effects.
\medterm{Proglottid}A segment of a tapeworm containing reproductive organs.
\medterm{Prognosis}The expected course and outcome of a disease or condition.
\medterm{Program}A planned set of activities or services, such as a screening or rehabilitation program.
\medterm{Progressive}Increasing or worsening over time, or advancing in a specified direction.
\medterm{Progressive Lenticular Degeneration}Wilson disease, an inherited copper-accumulation disorder affecting the liver, brain, and eyes.
\medterm{Progressive Muscular Atrophy}A motor-neuron disorder predominantly affecting lower motor neurons and causing progressive muscle wasting and weakness.
\medterm{Projection}The act of extending, displaying, or estimating something; in imaging, a view produced by directing rays through a body.
\medterm{Prolactin}A pituitary hormone that stimulates breast development and milk production and influences reproductive function.
\medterm{Prolactinoma}A usually benign pituitary adenoma that secretes excess prolactin.
\medterm{Prolapse}Descent or displacement of an organ from its normal position.
\medterm{Proliferation}Rapid increase in the number of cells or organisms.
\medterm{Proliferative}Characterized by cell multiplication or new tissue growth.
\medterm{Proline}A protein-forming amino acid with a cyclic side chain.
\medterm{Promazine}A phenothiazine antipsychotic with sedative properties.
\medterm{Promontory}A projecting anatomical prominence, such as the sacral or cochlear promontory.
\medterm{Promoters}DNA regions that initiate transcription of a gene when bound by regulatory proteins.
\medterm{Pronation}Rotation of the forearm so the palm faces posteriorly or downward, or a related foot movement.
\medterm{Pronephros}The earliest embryonic kidney structure, which is nonfunctional in humans and largely regresses.
\medterm{Pronator}A muscle that turns a limb or part of it into a pronated position.
\medterm{Prontosil}An early sulfonamide antibacterial drug, historically important in antimicrobial therapy.
\medterm{Propanolol}A nonselective beta-adrenergic blocker used for hypertension, arrhythmias, angina, tremor, migraine prevention, and other indications.
\medterm{Propantheline}An antimuscarinic medicine formerly used for gastrointestinal spasm or excessive sweating.
\medterm{Prophylaxis}Treatment or preventive measures intended to reduce the risk of disease or infection.
\medterm{Propofol}A short-acting intravenous anesthetic used for induction and maintenance of anesthesia and procedural sedation.
\medterm{Propranolol}A nonselective beta blocker used for cardiovascular conditions, tremor, migraine prevention, and other indications.
\medterm{Proprioception}Awareness of body position and movement from receptors in muscles, tendons, joints, and skin.
\medterm{Proprioceptor}A sensory receptor that detects position, movement, or muscle stretch.
\medterm{Propulsion}The act of driving or moving something forward; in Parkinson disease, propulsion may be impaired.
\medterm{Proptosis}Forward displacement or bulging of an eye.
\medterm{Propylthiouracil}An antithyroid medicine that inhibits thyroid hormone synthesis and peripheral conversion of T4 to T3.
\medterm{Prosopagnosia}Inability to recognize familiar faces despite otherwise adequate vision.
\medterm{Prostaglandins}Locally acting lipid mediators involved in pain, inflammation, vascular tone, gastric protection, and reproductive physiology.
\medterm{Prostate}A gland below the bladder surrounding the proximal urethra that contributes fluid to semen.
\medterm{Prostatectomy}Surgical removal of part or all of the prostate.
\medterm{Prostate Gland}The prostate, a gland below the bladder that surrounds the urethra and contributes secretions to semen.
\medterm{Prostate-Specific Antigen (PSA)}A kallikrein-related enzyme measured in blood to assess prostate disorders, with interpretation affected by benign and malignant conditions.
\medterm{Prostatism}Lower urinary-tract symptoms caused by bladder-outlet obstruction or dysfunction, often associated with prostate enlargement.
\medterm{Prostatitis}Inflammation or infection of the prostate, causing pelvic pain, urinary symptoms, or systemic illness.
\medterm{Prosthesis}An artificial device replacing or supporting a missing body part or function.
\medterm{Prosthetic}Relating to or made by a prosthesis.
\medterm{Prostrate}Lying flat or face downward; not to be confused with prostate.
\medterm{Protamine}A strongly basic protein used to reverse the anticoagulant effect of heparin.
\medterm{Protease}An enzyme that breaks down proteins by cleaving peptide bonds.
\medterm{Protective}Serving to prevent or reduce injury, infection, or other harm.
\medterm{Protein}A molecule made of amino acids that performs structural, enzymatic, transport, signaling, or immune functions.
\medterm{Proteinuria}Abnormally high protein excretion in urine, often indicating kidney disease but also occurring transiently.
\medterm{Proteolysis}Breakdown of proteins into smaller peptides or amino acids.
\medterm{Proteolytic}Relating to or causing protein breakdown.
\medterm{Proteus}A genus of gram-negative bacteria that can cause urinary and wound infections.
\medterm{Prothrombin}A vitamin-K-dependent plasma protein converted to thrombin during blood coagulation.
\medterm{Prothrombin Time}A blood test assessing the extrinsic and common coagulation pathways, commonly reported as INR.
\medterm{Protozoa}Single-celled eukaryotic organisms, some of which cause human disease.
\medterm{Protractile}Capable of being extended or protruded.
\medterm{Protractor}An instrument or muscle that moves a structure forward or outward.
\medterm{Protrusion}A part that projects outward or a disc or tissue displaced beyond its normal boundary.
\medterm{Provisional Diagnosis}A working diagnosis made before all information is available and subject to revision.
\medterm{Provocation}An action or stimulus that triggers a symptom, response, or test finding.
\medterm{Proximal}Closer to the point of attachment or to the center of the body than another structure.
\medterm{Prurigo}A group of intensely itchy skin disorders with papules or nodules caused by inflammation and scratching.
\medterm{Pruritus}Itching, an unpleasant skin sensation that creates the desire to scratch.
\medterm{Prussian Blue}A blue pigment used in laboratory staining and as an antidote for selected radioactive cesium or thallium poisoning.
\medterm{Psalterium}The body of the fornix in the brain, also called the hippocampal commissure; historically, a book of psalms.
\medterm{Psammoma}A tumor or lesion containing concentrically laminated calcified bodies called psammoma bodies.
\medterm{Pscyhiatry}A misspelling of psychiatry, the medical specialty concerned with mental disorders.
\medterm{Pseudarthrosis}Failure of a fracture to unite, producing a false joint or persistent abnormal mobility.
\medterm{Pseudo-}A combining form meaning false or resembling but not truly.
\medterm{Pseudocyesis}A false belief or physical state resembling pregnancy without a fetus.
\medterm{Pseudodementia}Cognitive symptoms caused by depression or another potentially reversible condition rather than primary neurodegeneration.
\medterm{Pseudohypertrophic Muscular Dystrophy}An older name for Duchenne or related muscular dystrophy with enlarged-appearing muscles caused by fat and connective tissue.
\medterm{Pseudomonas}A genus of gram-negative bacteria, including Pseudomonas aeruginosa, that can cause opportunistic infections.
\medterm{Pseudoneurosis}An obsolete term for symptoms resembling neurosis but caused by an underlying physical disorder.
\medterm{Pseudorabies}Aujeszky disease, a herpesvirus infection of animals; humans are rare accidental hosts.
\medterm{Pseudoxanthoma Elasticum}An inherited connective-tissue disorder with mineralization and fragmentation of elastic fibers, affecting skin, eyes, and blood vessels.
\medterm{Psittacosis}A zoonotic infection caused by Chlamydia psittaci, usually acquired from infected birds.
\medterm{Psoas}A major hip-flexor muscle running from the lumbar spine to the femur.
\medterm{Psoriasis}A chronic immune-mediated skin disease producing sharply demarcated, often scaly plaques.
\medterm{Psychasthenia}An obsolete term for obsessive doubts, compulsive thoughts, or an inability to direct attention.
\medterm{Psychedelic}Producing altered perception, mood, and cognition, as with certain hallucinogenic substances.
\medterm{Psychiatry}The medical specialty concerned with mental health and the diagnosis and treatment of mental disorders.
\medterm{Psychic}Relating to the mind or mental processes; claims of supernatural perception are not medical findings.
\medterm{Psychoactive}Affecting mood, thought, perception, behavior, or other mental processes.
\medterm{Psychoanalysis}A historical psychological theory and treatment approach emphasizing unconscious processes and conflicts.
\medterm{Psychodrama}A therapeutic method using guided dramatic enactment to explore emotions, relationships, and behavior.
\medterm{Psychological}Relating to mental processes, emotions, behavior, or their effects on health.
\medterm{Psychology}The scientific study of behavior and mental processes.
\medterm{Psychometrics}The measurement of psychological characteristics using standardized tests and statistical methods.
\medterm{Psychomotor}Relating to the interaction of mental processes and physical movement.
\medterm{Psychoneurosis}An obsolete term for a nonpsychotic emotional disorder, historically including anxiety and phobic conditions.
\medterm{Psychopathic}Relating to severe, persistent antisocial personality traits; the term is often used imprecisely.
\medterm{Psychopathology}The study or description of abnormal mental states and behavior.
\medterm{Psychopharmacology}The study of how drugs affect behavior, mood, cognition, and other mental processes.
\medterm{Psychosexual}Relating to psychological and sexual development or functioning.
\medterm{Psychosis}A state involving impaired reality testing, such as delusions, hallucinations, or disorganized thought.
\medterm{Psychosurgery}Surgery intended to alter brain circuits for severe, treatment-resistant psychiatric illness; it is rarely used and highly specialized.
\medterm{Psychotherapy}Structured psychological treatment using therapeutic communication and evidence-based techniques.
\medterm{Psychotic}Relating to or characterized by psychosis.
\medterm{Pterygium}A wing-shaped growth of conjunctival tissue extending onto the cornea, usually related to ultraviolet exposure.
\medterm{Ptilosis}Abnormal growth, loss, or condition of hair or feathers; the exact meaning depends on context.
\medterm{Ptosis}Drooping of an eyelid or another body structure.
\medterm{Ptyalin}An older name for salivary amylase, the enzyme that begins starch digestion in the mouth.
\medterm{Ptyalism}Excessive salivation.
\medterm{Puberty}The developmental period in which hormonal changes produce sexual maturation and reproductive capability.
\medterm{Pubes}The pubic region or pubic hair.
\medterm{Pubic}Relating to the pubis or the region over the front of the pelvis.
\medterm{Pubis}The front part of the pelvic bone, meeting its opposite side at the pubic symphysis.
\medterm{Pudendal}Relating to the external genitalia or perineum.
\medterm{Puerperal}Relating to the period after childbirth, usually the first six weeks.
\medterm{Puerperal Fever}Fever and infection occurring after childbirth, often from uterine or genital-tract infection.
\medterm{Puerperium}The approximately six-week period after childbirth during which the reproductive organs return toward their nonpregnant state.
\medterm{Pulex}A genus of fleas, including Pulex irritans.
\medterm{Pulex Irritans}The human flea, which can cause bites and occasionally transmit infections.
\medterm{Pulmonary Artery}The artery carrying deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle to the lungs.
\medterm{Pulmonary Embolism}Blockage of a pulmonary artery, usually by a clot that traveled from a deep vein.
\medterm{Pulmonary Oedema}Accumulation of fluid in lung tissue or alveoli, causing breathlessness and impaired gas exchange.
\medterm{Pulmonary Stenosis}Narrowing of the pulmonary valve or outflow tract, obstructing blood flow from the right ventricle.
\medterm{Pulmonary Tuberculosis}Tuberculosis infection involving the lungs, caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis.
\medterm{Pulp}The soft vascular and nervous tissue inside a tooth or, in plants, soft internal tissue.
\medterm{Pulpitis} Inflammation of the dental pulp, usually caused by caries, trauma, cracks, or restorative treatment. Reversible pulpitis causes brief pain provoked by cold or sweet stimuli; irreversible pulpitis causes spontaneous or lingering pain and may progress to pulp necrosis, requiring endodontic treatment or extraction.
\medterm{Pulse}The rhythmic expansion of an artery produced by each heartbeat.
\medterm{Pulseless Disease}Takayasu arteritis, a large-vessel vasculitis that can reduce pulses in affected arteries.
\medterm{Pulsus}A Latin term for pulse, used in names describing abnormal pulse patterns.
\medterm{Pumice}A porous volcanic stone used as an abrasive; excessive dust inhalation can irritate the lungs.
\medterm{Puna}A high-altitude region or, in some sources, a term related to altitude sickness; precise meaning depends on context.
\medterm{Punch-Drunk}An obsolete term for chronic traumatic encephalopathy associated with repeated head injury, especially in boxing.
\medterm{Punctate}Composed of or marked by small spots or points.
\medterm{Puncture}A wound or procedure made by a pointed object or needle.
\medterm{Pungent}Having a strong, sharp smell or taste; pungent substances may irritate mucous membranes.
\medterm{Punicin}A rare or historical term whose precise medical meaning is source-dependent and should be verified.
\medterm{Pupil}The central opening in the iris through which light enters the eye.
\medterm{Pupillary}Relating to the pupil.
\medterm{Purge}To empty the bowel, stomach, or body of a substance; the term may also mean forceful vomiting or diarrhea.
\medterm{Purgative}A substance that induces bowel evacuation.
\medterm{Purine}A nitrogen-containing compound forming part of nucleotides; its breakdown produces uric acid.
\medterm{Purkinje Fibres}Specialized cardiac conduction fibers that rapidly distribute impulses through the ventricular myocardium.
\medterm{Purpura}Purple or red nonblanching skin or mucosal discoloration caused by bleeding into tissue.
\medterm{Pus}A thick fluid containing dead cells, bacteria, inflammatory cells, and tissue debris.
\medterm{Pustule}A small, raised skin lesion filled with pus.
\medterm{Putrefaction}Decomposition of tissues after death by bacterial and enzymatic processes.
\medterm{Putrescine}A foul-smelling diamine produced during breakdown of proteins in decaying tissue.
\medterm{Pyelitis}Inflammation of the renal pelvis, usually due to infection.
\medterm{Pyelogram}An imaging record of the renal pelvis and ureters after contrast administration.
\medterm{Pyelography}Imaging of the renal pelvis and ureters using contrast.
\medterm{Pyelolithotomy}Surgical removal of a stone from the renal pelvis.
\medterm{Pyelonephritis}Infection and inflammation of the kidney and renal pelvis, usually caused by ascending bacteria.
\medterm{Pyloric Stenosis}Narrowing of the pyloric outlet, classically causing projectile vomiting in infants or gastric-outlet symptoms in adults.
\medterm{Pyloroplasty}Surgical widening of the pylorus to improve gastric emptying.
\medterm{Pylorus}The distal part of the stomach leading into the duodenum.
\medterm{Pyoderma}A bacterial skin infection producing pus, crusts, erosions, or ulcers.
\medterm{Pyogenesis}Formation of pus.
\medterm{Pyogenic}Producing pus or associated with pus-forming infection.
\medterm{Pyometra}Accumulation of pus within the uterus.
\medterm{Pyosalpinx}Accumulation of pus within a fallopian tube, usually due to pelvic infection.
\medterm{Pyramid}A pyramid-shaped structure; in neuroanatomy, the medullary pyramids contain corticospinal fibers.
\medterm{Pyramidal Tracts}Descending motor pathways from the cerebral cortex to the brainstem and spinal cord.
\medterm{Pyramidon}An obsolete analgesic and antipyretic medicine, also called aminopyrine.
\medterm{Pyrazinamide}An antituberculous medicine used in combination therapy, especially during the initial phase.
\medterm{Pyrethrins}Natural insecticides derived from chrysanthemum flowers; exposure can irritate skin and airways.
\medterm{Pyrethrum}A plant-derived insecticide source containing pyrethrins.
\medterm{Pyrexia}Fever or an elevated body temperature.
\medterm{Pyridostigmine}An acetylcholinesterase inhibitor used mainly for myasthenia gravis and selected neuromuscular disorders.
\medterm{Pyrimethamine}An antiparasitic medicine that inhibits folate metabolism and is used for selected protozoal infections.
\medterm{Pyrosis}Heartburn, a burning discomfort behind the breastbone caused commonly by reflux of gastric contents.
\medterm{Pyuria}Presence of white blood cells or pus in urine, often indicating urinary-tract inflammation or infection.

\synonyms
Artificial pacemaker

\medterm{Pacemaker, Natural} The sinoatrial node, whose cells normally initiate the heartbeat.

\synonyms
Sinoatrial node

\medterm{PAD} Peripheral artery disease caused by narrowed arteries, usually affecting the legs.

\synonyms
Peripheral artery disease

\medterm{Painkillers} Medicines or other treatments that relieve pain.

\synonyms
Analgesics

\medterm{Palpate} To examine body tissues by carefully feeling them with the hands.

\synonyms
Palpation

\medterm{PCP} Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (formerly Pneumocystis carinii pneumonia); opportunistic infection in immunocompromised patients, especially transplant recipients.

\medterm{Pelvic Floor} A group of muscles and connective tissues supporting the pelvic organs and controlling urinary and fecal continence.

\medterm{Pepsin} A stomach enzyme that begins digestion of proteins.

\medterm{Percutaneous Electrical Nerve Stimulation} Electrical stimulation delivered through the skin to reduce pain signals.

\synonyms
PENS

\medterm{Percutaneous Transluminal Coronary Angioplasty} Balloon-based treatment that widens a narrowed coronary artery, often followed by stent placement.

\synonyms
PTCA; balloon angioplasty

\medterm{Performance Anxiety} Anxiety about sexual performance that can interfere with sexual function.

\medterm{Periodic Limb Movement Disorder} Repeated involuntary limb movements during sleep that may disrupt rest.

\synonyms
PLMD

\medterm{Periodization} Planned variation of exercise volume and intensity over time.

\medterm{Peripheral Vascular Disease} Disease involving narrowed or blocked blood vessels outside the heart and brain.

\synonyms
Peripheral arterial disease

\medterm{Peripheral Vision} The portion of the visual field outside the direct line of sight.

\synonyms
Side vision

\medterm{Personal Emergency Response System} A wearable or nearby device that lets a person summon help during an emergency.

\synonyms
Medical alert system

\medterm{Pessimism} A tendency to expect negative outcomes or interpret situations unfavorably.

\medterm{Peyronie’s Disease} Scar tissue within the penis that can cause curvature and pain during erection.

\synonyms
Peyronie disease

\medterm{Phantom Limb} The sensation that an amputated limb is still present, sometimes with pain.

\synonyms
Phantom-limb sensation

\medterm{Phlebotomy} The collection of blood from a vein, usually with a needle.

\synonyms
Venipuncture

\medterm{Photoreceptors} Specialized retinal cells that detect light; rods and cones are the two main types.

\medterm{Physician Assistant} A licensed clinician who evaluates patients and provides medical care under a physician’s practice framework.

\synonyms
PA

\medterm{Pigment} A substance that gives tissue, hair, skin, or other material its color.

\medterm{Piles} Enlarged or swollen veins in the anus or lower rectum.

\synonyms
Hemorrhoids

\medterm{Pill} A solid oral dosage form containing medicine or another active ingredient.

\synonyms
Tablet

\medterm{Plasma} The liquid portion of blood in which blood cells and dissolved substances circulate.

\medterm{Plasma Cells} Mature B lymphocytes that produce antibodies.

\medterm{Platelets} Small blood components that gather at damaged vessels and help form clots.

\synonyms
Thrombocytes

\medterm{Polymenorrhea} Menstrual bleeding occurring at unusually short intervals.

\medterm{Polyp} A growth projecting from a mucous membrane; it may be benign, precancerous, or rarely cancerous.

\medterm{Positive Psychology} The study of well-being, strengths, resilience, and factors that help people thrive.

\medterm{Posterior Chamber} The part of the eye between the iris and lens, containing aqueous humor.

\medterm{Posterior Heel Bursitis} Inflammation of a bursa near the back of the heel, causing pain and swelling.

\synonyms
Retrocalcaneal bursitis

\medterm{Posterior Keyhole Foraminotomy} A minimally invasive operation that enlarges a spinal nerve opening to relieve compression.

\medterm{Postpartum} Relating to the period after childbirth.

\synonyms
Postnatal

\medterm{Post-Traumatic Headache} A headache that begins or persists after a head or neck injury.

\medterm{Power} The rate at which work is performed; in exercise, it combines force and movement speed.

\medterm{Power Training} Exercise designed to improve the ability to produce force rapidly.

\synonyms
Explosive training

\medterm{Prazosin} An alpha-1 blocker that relaxes blood vessels and smooth muscle.

\medterm{Precursor} A substance that can be converted into another biologically active substance.

\medterm{Prehypertension} Blood pressure above normal but below the former threshold for hypertension.

\synonyms
Elevated blood pressure

\medterm{Preload Reduction} Lowering the amount of blood returning to the heart to reduce cardiac workload.

\medterm{Premature Ventricular Contraction} An early heartbeat arising from the ventricles.

\synonyms
PVC

\medterm{Prick Test} A skin test in which small amounts of suspected allergens are introduced into the skin.

\synonyms
Skin-prick test

\medterm{Primary Angioplasty} Angioplasty used as the initial treatment to reopen an artery during an acute heart attack.

\medterm{Primary Osteoporosis} Bone loss due to aging, menopause, or other normal physiological processes rather than another disease.

\medterm{Procurement} Process of recovering organs and tissues from donors for transplantation.

\medterm{Protocol} A predefined plan specifying procedures for a clinical study, examination, or treatment.

\medterm{PSA} Prostate-specific antigen, a protein measured in blood to help evaluate prostate conditions.

\synonyms
Prostate-specific antigen

\medterm{PSA Velocity} The rate at which PSA concentration changes over time.

\medterm{Psychiatrist} Physician specializing in diagnosis and treatment of mental disorders; involved in pre-transplant psychological evaluation and post-transplant mental health management.

\medterm{Psychogenic} Caused or substantially influenced by psychological factors.

\medterm{Psychogenic Erectile Dysfunction} Erectile difficulty primarily related to psychological factors rather than a structural disease.

\medterm{Psychosomatic} Relating to physical symptoms influenced by psychological processes or stress.

\medterm{PTCA} Percutaneous transluminal coronary angioplasty, a procedure that opens a narrowed coronary artery.

\synonyms
Percutaneous coronary intervention

\medterm{PTLD} Post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder; a spectrum of lymphoid proliferations ranging from benign hyperplasia to lymphoma, occurring in immunosuppressed transplant recipients.

\medterm{Puborectalis Muscle} A pelvic-floor muscle sling around the rectum that helps maintain continence.

\medterm{Pudendal Arteries} Arteries supplying blood to the external genital and perineal region.

\medterm{Pudendal Nerve} The nerve carrying sensation and motor signals to much of the perineum and pelvic floor.

\medterm{Pulmonary} Relating to the lungs.

\medterm{Pulmonary Veins} Veins carrying oxygen-rich blood from the lungs to the left atrium.

\medterm{Pulse Pressure} The difference between systolic and diastolic blood pressure.

\medterm{Pump Failure} Severe weakening of the heart’s pumping ability, causing inadequate blood flow to organs.

\synonyms
Cardiogenic pump failure

\medterm{Purulent} Containing or producing pus.

\medterm{Pyloric Sphincter} A muscular valve regulating passage of stomach contents into the duodenum.

\synonyms
Pylorus

\medterm{Pyridoxine} A form of vitamin B6 involved in metabolism and nervous-system function.

\synonyms
Vitamin B6

\medterm{P2Y12 Inhibitor} An antiplatelet drug that blocks the platelet P2Y12 ADP receptor, reducing activation
and aggregation. Clopidogrel, prasugrel, and ticagrelor are used in selected acute
coronary syndrome and percutaneous coronary intervention settings, with bleeding and
drug-specific contraindications considered.

\medterm{p53 Tumor Suppressor} The p53 protein, encoded by the TP53 gene on chromosome 17p, is a transcription factor
and the most commonly mutated gene in human cancer, with mutations found in over 50
percent of all malignancies.

\medterm{Pacemaker} An implanted electronic device that delivers electrical impulses to the heart to
regulate heart rate and rhythm. Indications include symptomatic bradycardia, high-degree
AV block, and sick sinus syndrome. Single-chamber pacemakers pace the atrium or
ventricle; dual-chamber pacemakers pace both.

\medterm{Pacemaker Complications} Early: pneumothorax, hematoma, cardiac perforation/tamponade, lead dislodgement. Late:
lead fracture, battery depletion, infection, tricuspid regurgitation from lead
interference, venous thrombosis, and pacemaker syndrome (AV dyssynchrony in VVI pacing).

\medterm{Pachygyria} A cortical malformation in which the cerebral gyri are abnormally broad and reduced in
number because of impaired neuronal migration. It may cause developmental delay,
intellectual disability, seizures, and motor or neurological impairment of variable
severity.

\medterm{Pachymeningitis} Inflammation of the dura mater (pachymeninx), the outermost layer of the meninges.
Pachymeningitis is less common than leptomeningitis (inflammation of pia and arachnoid
mater).

\medterm{Pacing} Planned regulation of activity, rest, and intensity to avoid symptom exacerbation while
gradually increasing tolerance. It is used in fatigue, chronic pain, cardiopulmonary
disease, and post-viral conditions.

\medterm{PACS} Picture Archiving and Communication System. Digital image storage and retrieval.

\medterm{PAD Management} Atherosclerotic occlusive disease of arteries supplying the lower limbs, causing reduced
blood flow. See peripheral arterial disease.

\medterm{Page kidney} Renal compression from a perinephric hematoma or mass causing hypertension through
activation of the renin-angiotensin system, typically following trauma or occurring in
patients on anticoagulation.

\medterm{Paget Disease of Bone} A chronic bone disorder characterized by excessive, disorganized bone remodeling with
increased osteoclastic bone resorption followed by compensatory osteoblastic bone
formation, producing structurally abnormal bone that is enlarged, weak, and prone to
fracture.

\medterm{Paget's Disease} A chronic bone disorder characterized by excessive, disorganized bone remodeling with
increased osteoclastic bone resorption followed by compensatory osteoblastic bone
formation, producing structurally abnormal bone that is enlarged, weak, and prone to
fracture. See Paget Disease of Bone.

\medterm{Paget Disease of the Nipple} A rare breast cancer involving the nipple and areola, often causing persistent eczema-like scaling, redness, itching, burning, or discharge. It may be associated with underlying ductal carcinoma in situ or invasive breast cancer.

\medterm{Pain} An unpleasant sensory and emotional experience associated with actual or potential
tissue damage, serving as a protective alerting system but becoming maladaptive when
chronic (lasting >3 months).

\medterm{Pain (Geriatric)} Pain in an older adult. Assessment considers communication, cognition, function, medicines, and possible causes; treatment is
individualized to the person and condition.

\synonyms
Geriatric

\medterm{Pain (Vascular)} Pain caused by impaired blood flow, often appearing in a limb with exertion as claudication or, in more severe disease, at rest.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Pain Assessment Scales} Pain assessment uses standardized scales to quantify pain intensity. Numeric rating
scale (NRS) asks patients to rate pain from 0 (no pain) to 10 (worst pain imaginable).
Visual analog scale (VAS) is a 10 cm line with anchors. Faces pain scale (Wong-Baker
FACES) uses facial expressions for children and cognitively impaired patients.

\medterm{Pain Management} Assessment and treatment of pain using appropriate pharmacologic, physical, psychological, and
interventional approaches. Treatment is individualized according to the cause, severity,
duration, functional effect, and risks of each option.

\medterm{Pain Relief (Analgesia)} Achieved through pharmacologic, interventional, physical, and psychologic modalities
targeting nociceptive transmission and pain perception.

\synonyms
Analgesia

\medterm{Pair Production} Photon interaction producing electron-positron pair. Requires energy >1.022 MeV.

\medterm{Paleness} A disease, symptom, syndrome, or clinical finding involving the body system or process named by the term; characteristic features, causes, severity, and complications guide diagnosis and management.

\medterm{Palindromic Rheumatism} Characterized by recurrent, self-limited attacks of mono- or oligoarthritis (hours to
days), periarticular soft tissue swelling, no radiographic erosions between attacks.
30-50\% evolve to RA (anti-CCP positive predicts progression).

\medterm{Palliative Care} Specialized medical care for serious illness focusing on symptom relief and quality of
life. Not limited to end-of-life. Can be provided alongside curative treatment.
Components: pain management, symptom control, psychosocial support, spiritual care,
advance care planning. Referral early in disease course.

\medterm{Palliative Rehabilitation} Rehabilitation for people with serious or life-limiting illness, focused on comfort,
function, participation, symptom management, and quality of life.

\medterm{Pallister-Hall Syndrome} A congenital disorder usually caused by pathogenic variants in GLI3. It is characterized
by hypothalamic hamartoma and mesoaxial polydactyly and may include imperforate anus,
renal or airway anomalies, and pituitary hormone abnormalities.

\medterm{Pallor} Paleness of the skin and mucous membranes, caused by reduced cutaneous blood flow
(vasoconstriction—cold, shock, anemia, fear) or reduced hemoglobin/pigmentation.

\medterm{Palmar erythema of pregnancy} Symmetrical redness of the palms occurring during pregnancy, thought to be related to
elevated circulating oestrogen levels causing vasodilatation. It is a benign,
physiological finding that typically resolves after delivery and requires no specific
treatment.

\medterm{Palmar Grasp Reflex} A primitive reflex in which an infant flexes the fingers around an object placed in the palm.
It is normally present at birth and gradually diminishes as voluntary grasp develops;
asymmetry or persistence may warrant neurologic assessment.

\medterm{Palpation} Examination by touching the body to assess tenderness, temperature, texture, masses, pulses, organ size, and movement.

\medterm{Palpebral Conjunctiva} The part of the conjunctiva that lines the inner surfaces of the eyelids. It joins the bulbar conjunctiva at the
fornices and helps protect and lubricate the eye.

\medterm{Palpitation} The subjective sensation of a rapid, forceful, or irregular heartbeat. Palpitations are
a common symptom with a broad differential.

\medterm{Palsy of the long thoracic nerve} Weakness or paralysis of the long thoracic nerve (C5-C7), which innervates the serratus
anterior muscle. Causes include trauma, compression, viral infection, or idiopathic.
Results in winging of the scapula, particularly when pushing against a wall, and
impaired overhead arm elevation. Most cases resolve spontaneously.

\medterm{Pancoast Syndrome} A clinical syndrome caused by an apical lung tumour invading the lower brachial plexus,
sympathetic chain, or adjacent structures. It may produce severe shoulder and arm pain,
hand weakness, sensory loss, and Horner syndrome.

\medterm{Pancoast Tumor} Superior sulcus tumor invading brachial plexus, subclavian vessels, and sympathetic
chain. Causes Horner syndrome, shoulder/arm pain, and hand muscle atrophy. Treated with
chemoradiation followed by surgery.

\medterm{Pancreas} Retroperitoneal gland (15-20 cm) behind the stomach, spanning from duodenum to spleen.
Exocrine function: acinar cells secrete digestive enzymes (amylase, lipase, proteases)
and bicarbonate into pancreatic duct to duodenum.

\medterm{Pancreas Divisum} A congenital anomaly in which the dorsal and ventral pancreatic ducts fail to fuse. Most
people are asymptomatic, but impaired drainage through the minor papilla can predispose
to recurrent pancreatitis.

\medterm{Pancreatectomy} Removal of all (total) or part of the pancreas. Pancreaticoduodenectomy (Whipple) is
used for pancreatic head and periampullary tumors; distal pancreatectomy for body and
tail lesions.

\medterm{Pancreatic Abscess} A localized collection of pus within or adjacent to the pancreas, usually developing as
a complication of infected pancreatic necrosis or severe pancreatitis. It may cause
persistent fever, abdominal pain, sepsis, and organ failure.

\medterm{Pancreatic Adenocarcinoma} A malignant tumor arising from the exocrine ductal cells of the pancreas. It may cause
jaundice, abdominal or back pain, weight loss, or no early symptoms; risk factors include
smoking, chronic pancreatitis, diabetes, obesity, and inherited cancer syndromes.

\medterm{Pancreatic Cancer} Malignancy arising in the pancreas, most often pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma. Symptoms may
include jaundice, abdominal or back pain, weight loss, or digestive problems, although early
disease may be asymptomatic. Treatment depends on tumor type, extent, and patient factors.

\medterm{Pancreatic Duct} The main duct of the pancreas, conveying exocrine pancreatic secretions to the duodenum.
It commonly joins the common bile duct before opening at the major duodenal papilla.

\medterm{Pancreatic Elastase} A stool test that measures elastase-1 as an indirect estimate of pancreatic exocrine
function. Low results support pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, but interpretation depends
on stool consistency, the assay, and the clinical setting. Replacement pancreatic enzymes
do not usually interfere with the test.

\medterm{Pancreatic Enzyme} Digestive enzymes secreted by pancreatic acinar cells into the duodenum, including
proteases (trypsinogen, chymotrypsinogen, proelastase, procarboxypeptidases), amylase
(starch digestion), and lipase (triglyceride digestion), along with bicarbonate from
ductal cells to neutralize gastric acid.

\medterm{Pancreatic Exocrine Insufficiency} Inadequate secretion or delivery of pancreatic digestive enzymes and bicarbonate,
causing maldigestion and malabsorption. Chronic pancreatitis, cystic fibrosis,
pancreatic resection, and advanced pancreatic disease are important causes; steatorrhea
and weight loss may respond to pancreatic enzyme replacement.

\medterm{Pancreatic Insufficiency} Inadequate production or delivery of pancreatic digestive enzymes, causing impaired digestion
